use numbering config with LevelFormat.BULLET\n   115\tconst doc = new Document({\n   116\t  numbering: {\n   117\t    config: [\n   118\t      { reference: \"bullets\",\n   119\t        levels: [{ level: 0, format: LevelFormat.BULLET, text: \"\u2022\", alignment: AlignmentType.LEFT,\n   120\t          style: { paragraph: { indent: { left: 720, hanging: 360 } } } }] },\n   121\t      { reference: \"numbers\",\n   122\t        levels: [{ level: 0, format: LevelFormat.DECIMAL, text: \"%1.\", alignment: AlignmentType.LEFT,\n   123\t          style: { paragraph: { indent: { left: 720, hanging: 360 } } } }] },\n   124\t    ]\n   125\t  },\n   126\t  sections: [{\n   127\t    children: [\n   128\t      new Paragraph({ numbering: { reference: \"bullets\", level: 0 },\n   129\t        children: [new TextRun(\"Bullet item\")] }),\n   130\t      new Paragraph({ numbering: { reference: \"numbers\", level: 0 },\n   131\t        children: [new TextRun(\"Numbered item\")] }),\n   132\t    ]\n   133\t  }]\n   134\t});\n   135\t\n   136\t// \u26a0\ufe0f Each reference creates INDEPENDENT numbering\n   137\t// Same reference = continues (1,2,3 then 4,5,6)\n   138\t// Different reference = restarts (1,2,3 then 1,2,3)\n   139\t```\n   140\t\n   141\t### Tables\n   142\t\n   143\t**CRITICAL: Tables need dual widths** - set both `columnWidths` on the table AND `width` on each cell. Without both, tables render incorrectly on some platforms.\n   144\t\n   145\t```javascript\n   146\t// CRITICAL: Always set table width for consistent rendering\n   147\t// CRITICAL: Use ShadingType.CLEAR (not SOLID) to prevent black backgrounds\n   148\tconst border = { style: BorderStyle.SINGLE, size: 1, color: \"CCCCCC\" };\n   149\tconst borders = { top: border, bottom: border, left: border, right: border };\n   150\t\n   151\tnew Table({\n   152\t  width: { size: 100, type: WidthType.PERCENTAGE }, // Always set table width\n   153\t  columnWidths: [4680, 4680], // Set at table level (DXA: 1440 = 1 inch)\n   154\t  rows: [\n   155\t    new TableRow({\n   156\t      children: [\n   157\t        new TableCell({\n   158\t          borders,\n   159\t          width: { size: 4680, type: WidthType.DXA }, // Also set on each cell\n   160\t          shading: { fill: \"D5E8F0\", type: ShadingType.CLEAR }, // CLEAR not SOLID\n   161\t          margins: { top: 80, bottom: 80, left: 120, right: 120 }, // Cell padding (internal, not added to width)\n   162\t          children: [new Paragraph({ children: [new TextRun(\"Cell\")] })]\n   163\t        })\n   164\t      ]\n   165\t    })\n   166\t  ]\n   167\t})\n   168\t```\n   169\t\n   170\t**Table width calculation:**\n   171\t\n   172\tUse `WidthType.PERCENTAGE` for simplicity, or `WidthType.DXA` for precise control:\n   173\t\n   174\t```javascript\n   175\t// Option 1: Percentage (recommended - automatically fits content area)\n   176\twidth: { size: 100, type: WidthType.PERCENTAGE }\n   177\t\n   178\t// Option 2: DXA (precise control)\n   179\t// Table width = sum of columnWidths = content width\n   180\t// US Letter with 1\" margins: 12240 - 2880 = 9360 DXA\n   181\twidth: { size: 9360, type: WidthType.DXA },\n   182\tcolumnWidths: [7000, 2360]  // Must sum to table width\n   183\t```\n   184\t\n   185\t**Width rules:**\n   186\t- Table width must equal the sum of `columnWidths`\n   187\t- Cell `width` must match corresponding `columnWidth`\n   188\t- Cell `margins` are internal padding - they reduce content area, not add to cell width\n   189\t- For full-width tables: use content width (page width minus left and right margins)\n   190\t\n   191\t### Images\n   192\t\n   193\t```javascript\n   194\t// CRITICAL: type parameter is REQUIRED\n   195\tnew Paragraph({\n   196\t  children: [new ImageRun({\n   197\t    type: \"png\", // Required: png, jpg, jpeg, gif, bmp, svg\n   198\t    data: fs.readFileSync(\"image.png\"),\n   199\t    transformation: { width: 200, height: 150 },\n   200\t    altText: { title: \"Title\", description: \"Desc\", name: \"Name\" } // All three required\n   201\t  })]\n   202\t})\n   203\t```\n   204\t\n   205\t### Page Breaks\n   206\t\n   207\t```javascript\n   208\t// CRITICAL: PageBreak must be inside a Paragraph\n   209\tnew Paragraph({ children: [new PageBreak()] })\n   210\t\n   211\t// Or use pageBreakBefore\n   212\tnew Paragraph({ pageBreakBefore: true, children: [new TextRun(\"New page\")] })\n   213\t```\n   214\t\n   215\t### Table of Contents\n   216\t\n   217\t```javascript\n   218\t// CRITICAL: Headings must use HeadingLevel ONLY - no custom styles\n   219\tnew TableOfContents(\"Table of Contents\", { hyperlink: true, headingStyleRange: \"1-3\" })\n   220\t```\n   221\t\n   222\t### Headers/Footers\n   223\t\n   224\t```javascript\n   225\tsections: [{\n   226\t  properties: {\n   227\t    page: { margin: { top: 1440, right: 1440, bottom: 1440, left: 1440 } } // 1440 = 1 inch\n   228\t  },\n   229\t  headers: {\n   230\t    default: new Header({ children: [new Paragraph({ children: [new TextRun(\"Header\")] })] })\n   231\t  },\n   232\t  footers: {\n   233\t    default: new Footer({ children: [new Paragraph({\n   234\t      children: [new TextRun(\"Page \"), new TextRun({ children: [PageNumber.CURRENT] })]\n   235\t    })] })\n   236\t  },\n   237\t  children: [/* content */]\n   238\t}]\n   239\t```\n   240\t\n   241\t### Critical Rules for docx-js\n   242\t\n   243\t- **Set page size explicitly** - docx-js defaults to A4; use US Letter (12240 x 15840 DXA) for US documents\n   244\t- **Never use `\\n`** - use separate Paragraph elements\n   245\t- **Never use unicode bullets** - use `LevelFormat.BULLET` with numbering config\n   246\t- **PageBreak must be in Paragraph** - standalone creates invalid XML\n   247\t- **ImageRun requires `type`** - always specify png/jpg/etc\n   248\t- **Always set table `width`** - use `{ size: 100, type: WidthType.PERCENTAGE }` for full width\n   249\t- **Tables need dual widths** - `columnWidths` array AND cell `width`, both must match\n   250\t- **Table width = sum of columnWidths** - for DXA, ensure they add up exactly\n   251\t- **Always add cell margins** - use `margins: { top: 80, bottom: 80, left: 120, right: 120 }` for readable padding\n   252\t- **Use `ShadingType.CLEAR`** - never SOLID for table shading\n   253\t- **TOC requires HeadingLevel only** - no custom styles on heading paragraphs\n   254\t- **Override built-in styles** - use exact IDs: \"Heading1\", \"Heading2\", etc.\n   255\t- **Include `outlineLevel`** - required for TOC (0 for H1, 1 for H2, etc.)\n   256\t\n   257\t---\n   258\t\n   259\t## Editing Existing Documents\n   260\t\n   261\t**Follow all 3 steps in order.**\n   262\t\n   263\t### Step 1: Unpack\n   264\t```bash\n   265\tpython scripts/unpack.py document.docx unpacked/\n   266\t```\n   267\tExtracts XML, pretty-prints, merges adjacent runs, and converts smart quotes to XML entities (`&#x201C;` etc.) so they survive editing. Use `--merge-runs false` to skip run merging.\n   268\t\n   269\t### Step 2: Edit XML\n   270\t\n   271\tEdit files in `unpacked/word/`. See XML Reference below for patterns.\n   272\t\n   273\t**Always use \"Claude\" as the author** for tracked changes and comments.\n   274\t\n   275\t**Use the Edit tool directly for string replacement. Do not write Python scripts.** Scripts introduce unnecessary complexity. The Edit tool shows exactly what is being replaced.\n   276\t\n   277\t**CRITICAL: Use smart quotes for new content.** When adding text with apostrophes or quotes, use XML entities to produce smart quotes:\n   278\t```xml\n   279\t<!-- Use these entities for professional typography -->\n   280\t<w:t>Here&#x2019;s a quote: &#x201C;Hello&#x201D;</w:t>\n   281\t```\n   282\t| Entity | Character |\n   283\t|--------|-----------|\n   284\t| `&#x2018;` | \u2018 (left single) |\n   285\t| `&#x2019;` | \u2019 (right single / apostrophe) |\n   286\t| `&#x201C;` | \u201c (left double) |\n   287\t| `&#x201D;` | \u201d (right double) |\n   288\t\n   289\t**Adding comments:** Use `comment.py` to handle boilerplate across multiple XML files (text must be pre-escaped XML):\n   290\t```bash\n   291\tpython scripts/comment.py unpacked/ 0 \"Comment text with &amp; and &#x2019;\"\n   292\tpython scripts/comment.py unpacked/ 1 \"Reply text\" --parent 0  # reply to comment 0\n   293\t```\n   294\tThen add markers to document.xml (see Comments in XML Reference).\n   295\t\n   296\t### Step 3: Pack\n   297\t```bash\n   298\tpython scripts/pack.py unpacked/ output.docx --original document.docx\n   299\t```\n   300\tValidates with auto-repair, condenses XML, and creates DOCX. Use `--validate false` to skip.\n   301\t\n   302\t**Auto-repair will fix:**\n   303\t- `durableId` >= 0x7FFFFFFF (regenerates valid ID)\n   304\t- Missing `xml:space=\"preserve\"` on `<w:t>` with whitespace\n   305\t\n   306\t**Auto-repair won't fix:**\n   307\t- Malformed XML, invalid element nesting, missing relationships, schema violations\n   308\t\n   309\t### Common Pitfalls\n   310\t\n   311\t- **Replace entire `<w:r>` elements**: When adding tracked changes, replace the whole `<w:r>...</w:r>` block with `<w:del>...<w:ins>...` as siblings. Don't inject tracked change tags inside a run.\n   312\t- **Preserve `<w:rPr>` formatting**: Copy the original run's `<w:rPr>` block into your tracked change runs to maintain bold, font size, etc.\n   313\t\n   314\t---\n   315\t\n   316\t## XML Reference\n   317\t\n   318\t### Schema Compliance\n   319\t\n   320\t- **Element order in `<w:pPr>`**: `<w:pStyle>`, `<w:numPr>`, `<w:spacing>`, `<w:ind>`, `<w:jc>`, `<w:rPr>` last\n   321\t- **Whitespace**: Add `xml:space=\"preserve\"` to `<w:t>` with leading/trailing spaces\n   322\t- **RSIDs**: Must be 8-digit hex (e.g., `00AB1234`)\n   323\t\n   324\t### Tracked Changes\n   325\t\n   326\t**Insertion:**\n   327\t```xml\n   328\t<w:ins w:id=\"1\" w:author=\"Claude\" w:date=\"2025-01-01T00:00:00Z\">\n   329\t  <w:r><w:t>inserted text</w:t></w:r>\n   330\t</w:ins>\n   331\t```\n   332\t\n   333\t**Deletion:**\n   334\t```xml\n   335\t<w:del w:id=\"2\" w:author=\"Claude\" w:date=\"2025-01-01T00:00:00Z\">\n   336\t  <w:r><w:delText>deleted text</w:delText></w:r>\n   337\t</w:del>\n   338\t```\n   339\t\n   340\t**Minimal edits** - only mark what changes:\n   341\t```xml\n   342\t<!-- Change \"30 days\" to \"60 days\" -->\n   343\t<w:r><w:t>The term is </w:t></w:r>\n   344\t<w:del w:id=\"1\" w:author=\"Claude\" w:date=\"...\">\n   345\t  <w:r><w:delText>30</w:delText></w:r>\n   346\t</w:del>\n   347\t<w:ins w:id=\"2\" w:author=\"Claude\" w:date=\"...\">\n   348\t  <w:r><w:t>60</w:t></w:r>\n   349\t</w:ins>\n   350\t<w:r><w:t> days.</w:t></w:r>\n   351\t```\n   352\t\n   353\t**Rejecting another author's insertion** - nest deletion inside their insertion:\n   354\t```xml\n   355\t<w:ins w:author=\"Jane\" w:id=\"5\">\n   356\t  <w:del w:author=\"Claude\" w:id=\"10\">\n   357\t    <w:r><w:delText>their inserted text</w:delText></w:r>\n   358\t  </w:del>\n   359\t</w:ins>\n   360\t```\n   361\t\n   362\t**Restoring another author's deletion** - add insertion after (don't modify their deletion):\n   363\t```xml\n   364\t<w:del w:author=\"Jane\" w:id=\"5\">\n   365\t  <w:r><w:delText>deleted text</w:delText></w:r>\n   366\t</w:del>\n   367\t<w:ins w:author=\"Claude\" w:id=\"10\">\n   368\t  <w:r><w:t>deleted text</w:t></w:r>\n   369\t</w:ins>\n   370\t```\n   371\t\n   372\t### Comments\n   373\t\n   374\tAfter running `comment.py` (see Step 2), add markers to document.xml. For replies, use `--parent` flag and nest markers inside the parent's:\n   375\t\n   376\t```xml\n   377\t<!-- Comment 0 with reply 1 nested inside -->\n   378\t<w:commentRangeStart w:id=\"0\"/>\n   379\t  <w:commentRangeStart w:id=\"1\"/>\n   380\t  <w:r><w:t>text</w:t></w:r>\n   381\t  <w:commentRangeEnd w:id=\"1\"/>\n   382\t<w:commentRangeEnd w:id=\"0\"/>\n   383\t<w:r><w:rPr><w:rStyle w:val=\"CommentReference\"/></w:rPr><w:commentReference w:id=\"0\"/></w:r>\n   384\t<w:r><w:rPr><w:rStyle w:val=\"CommentReference\"/></w:rPr><w:commentReference w:id=\"1\"/></w:r>\n   385\t```\n   386\t\n   387\t### Images\n   388\t\n   389\t1. Add image file to `word/media/`\n   390\t2. without explicitly naming. This paper addresses this critical gap by investigating whether Large Language Models (LLMs) can serve as implicit concept recognizers for knowledge graph linking. Through systematic experiments across five domains (mathematics, algorithms, economics, game theory, and quantum physics), we demonstrate that LLMs achieve 100% recall on core implicit concepts while maintaining 96% overall accuracy. We propose a hybrid architecture where LLMs perform semantic concept recognition and knowledge base APIs provide precise identifier mapping. Our findings reveal that the bottleneck in concept-to-knowledge-graph linking lies not in semantic understanding\u2014which LLMs handle effectively\u2014but in arbitrary identifier recall, which API lookup resolves. This work opens new possibilities for comprehensive text-to-knowledge-graph mapping that captures both what texts say and what they mean.\"\n      ]),\n      \n      new Paragraph({ children: [new PageBreak()] }),\n      \n      // 1. Introduction\n      h1(\"1. Introduction\"),\n      p([\n        \"Entity linking\u2014the task of connecting textual mentions to unique identifiers in knowledge bases\u2014has become fundamental to knowledge-intensive NLP applications. Systems like FALCON, mGENRE, and spaCy-entity-linker successfully map explicit entity mentions (\\\"Barack Obama,\\\" \\\"Paris,\\\" \\\"Apple Inc.\\\") to their corresponding Wikidata Q-identifiers. However, these systems share a critical limitation: they can only recognize concepts that texts \",\n        italic(\"explicitly mention\"),\n        \".\"\n      ]),\n      p(\"Consider the following mathematical proof text:\"),\n      p([\n        { text: \"\\\"Suppose \u221a2 = p/q where p and q are coprime. Then 2q\u00b2 = p\u00b2, so p\u00b2 is even, thus p is even. Let p = 2k, then q\u00b2 = 2k\u00b2, so q is also even. But this contradicts our assumption that p and q are coprime. Therefore \u221a2 is irrational.\\\"\", italics: true }\n      ]),\n      p([\n        \"A human reader immediately recognizes that this text \",\n        italic(\"embodies\"),\n        \" proof by contradiction (Q184899 in Wikidata)\u2014yet the phrase \\\"proof by contradiction\\\" never appears. Current entity linking systems, which rely on surface form matching, cannot detect such implicit concepts. They might identify \\\"\u221a2\\\" or \\\"irrational,\\\" but they miss the methodological essence of the text.\"\n      ]),\n      p(\"This gap between explicit mention recognition and implicit concept understanding represents a fundamental limitation in current knowledge graph linking approaches. We term this the Implicit Concept Recognition Problem (ICRP):\"),\n      p([\n        { text: \"Definition (ICRP): \", bold: true },\n        \"Given a text T that embodies concept C without explicitly naming C, map T to the knowledge base identifier for C.\"\n      ]),\n      p(\"This paper makes the following contributions:\"),\n      p([\n        { text: \"1. Problem Formalization: \", bold: true },\n        \"We formally distinguish between explicit entity linking (solved) and implicit concept recognition (unsolved), establishing ICRP as a distinct research problem.\"\n      ]),\n      p([\n        { text: \"2. LLM Capability Assessment: \", bold: true },\n        \"Through controlled experiments, we demonstrate that LLMs can accurately identify implicit concepts in natural language (achieving 100% recall on core concepts) but cannot reliably produce knowledge base identifiers directly.\"\n      ]),\n      p([\n        { text: \"3. Hybrid Architecture: \", bold: true },\n        \"We propose and validate a two-stage architecture: LLM \u2192 Concept Names \u2192 API \u2192 Identifiers, which combines LLM semantic understanding with API precision.\"\n      ]),\n      p([\n        { text: \"4. Empirical Validation: \", bold: true },\n        \"We present experiments across five diverse domains demonstrating consistent implicit concept recognition with 96% average accuracy.\"\n      ]),\n\n      // 2. Related Work\n      h1(\"2. Related Work\"),\n      \n      h2(\"2.1 Entity Linking Systems\"),\n      p([\n        \"Entity linking research has produced sophisticated systems for connecting text to knowledge bases. AIDA (Hoffart et al., 2011) pioneered joint entity disambiguation using coherence graphs. More recent neural approaches include BLINK (Wu et al., 2020), which uses bi-encoder architecture for candidate retrieval, and mGENRE (De Cao et al., 2022), which generates entity names autoregressively across 100+ languages. FALCON 2.0 (Sakor et al., 2020) specifically targets Wikidata, combining rule-based and neural methods for entity and relation linking.\"\n      ]),\n      p([\n        \"Despite these advances, all existing systems share a common assumption: the target concept must have a \",\n        italic(\"surface form\"),\n        \" in the text that can be matched against knowledge base entries. This assumption fundamentally limits their scope to explicit mentions.\"\n      ]),\n      \n      h2(\"2.2 Concept Extraction and Topic Modeling\"),\n      p(\"Topic modeling approaches (LDA, neural topic models) extract latent themes from documents but produce distributional representations rather than discrete knowledge base links. Concept extraction systems identify domain concepts but typically rely on terminology matching or ontology-guided recognition, still requiring explicit textual anchors.\"),\n      \n      h2(\"2.3 Knowledge Graph Population\"),\n      p([\n        \"Knowledge base population (KBP) tasks extend entity linking to relation extraction and slot filling. TAC-KBP benchmarks (Ji et al., 2010) evaluate systems on extracting structured facts from text. However, these tasks focus on \",\n        italic(\"extracting stated facts\"),\n        \" rather than \",\n        italic(\"inferring unstated concepts\"),\n        \".\"\n      ]),\n      \n      h2(\"2.4 The Gap: Implicit Concept Recognition\"),\n      p([\n        \"To our knowledge, no existing system addresses the task of linking texts to concepts they \",\n        italic(\"embody but do not name\"),\n        \". This gap is significant because much of what texts communicate is implicit\u2014a proof demonstrates a method, a narrative illustrates a pattern, a description instantiates a principle. Current NLP systems miss this semantic layer entirely.\"\n      ]),\n\n      // 3. Problem Formulation\n      h1(\"3. Problem Formulation\"),\n      \n      h2(\"3.1 Explicit vs. Implicit Concepts\"),\n      p(\"We distinguish two types of text-concept relationships:\"),\n      p([\n        { text: \"Explicit Concepts: \", bold: true },\n        \"A concept C is explicit in text T if T contains a surface form S that conventionally refers to C. Example: \\\"Einstein\\\" in \\\"Einstein developed relativity\\\" explicitly refers to Q937.\"\n      ]),\n      p([\n        { text: \"Implicit Concepts: \", bold: true },\n        \"A concept C is implicit in text T if T instantiates, demonstrates, or embodies C without containing any conventional surface form for C. Example: The \u221a2 proof above embodies \\\"proof by contradiction\\\" (Q184899) without mentioning it.\"\n      ]),\n      p(\"This distinction is not merely terminological\u2014it reflects fundamentally different recognition mechanisms:\"),\n      \n      createTable(\n        [\"Dimension\", \"Explicit Concepts\", \"Implicit Concepts\"],\n        [\n          [\"Recognition mechanism\", \"Surface form matching\", \"Semantic pattern recognition\"],\n          [\"Knowledge required\", \"Mention-entity dictionary\", \"Conceptual understanding\"],\n          [\"Current system capability\", \"High (solved problem)\", \"Near zero (unsolved)\"],\n          [\"Wikidata examples\", \"Q937 (Einstein)\", \"Q184899 (proof by contradiction)\"]\n        ]\n      ),\n      new Paragraph({ spacing: { after: 200 } }),\n      \n      h2(\"3.2 The Identifier Precision Problem\"),\n      p(\"A secondary challenge emerges even when implicit concepts are recognized: mapping concept names to precise knowledge base identifiers. Wikidata's Q-identifiers are arbitrary numeric codes (Q184899 for \\\"proof by contradiction\\\") with no semantic relationship to concept meaning. This creates a \\\"last mile\\\" problem:\"),\n      p([\n        \"Even if a system correctly identifies that a text embodies \\\"proof by contradiction,\\\" it must still map this natural language concept to the specific identifier Q184899\u2014a task requiring either memorization of arbitrary mappings or real-time knowledge base lookup.\"\n      ]),\n      \n      h2(\"3.3 Research Questions\"),\n      p(\"This work addresses three research questions:\"),\n      p([\n        { text: \"RQ1: \", bold: true },\n        \"Can Large Language Models recognize implicit concepts that texts embody but do not explicitly name?\"\n      ]),\n      p([\n        { text: \"RQ2: \", bold: true },\n        \"Can LLMs directly produce knowledge base identifiers (Q-numbers) for recognized concepts?\"\n      ]),\n      p([\n        { text: \"RQ3: \", bold: true },\n        \"What architecture best combines LLM semantic understanding with knowledge base precision?\"\n      ]),\n\n      new Paragraph({ children: [new PageBreak()] }),\n      \n      // 4. Methodology\n      h1(\"4. Methodology\"),\n      \n      h2(\"4.1 Experimental Design\"),\n      p(\"To assess LLM capabilities for implicit concept recognition, we designed a controlled experiment with five test cases spanning diverse domains:\"),\n      \n      createTable(\n        [\"Test\", \"Domain\", \"Input Text\", \"Target Implicit Concepts\"],\n        [\n          [\"T1\", \"Mathematics\", \"\u221a2 irrationality proof\", \"proof by contradiction, reductio ad absurdum\"],\n          [\"T2\", \"Algorithms\", \"Quicksort description\", \"quicksort, divide and conquer, recursion\"],\n          [\"T3\", \"Economics\", \"Price-wage cycle\", \"wage-price spiral, cost-push inflation\"],\n          [\"T4\", \"Game Theory\", \"Prisoner scenario\", \"prisoner's dilemma, Nash equilibrium\"],\n          [\"T5\", \"Physics\", \"Double-slit observation\", \"wave-particle duality, quantum measurement\"]\n        ]\n      ),\n      new Paragraph({ spacing: { after: 200 } }),\n      \n      p([\n        \"Each test case was constructed to describe a well-known phenomenon or method \",\n        italic(\"without using its standard name\"),\n        \". For example, T1 presents a complete proof by contradiction but never uses the phrase \\\"proof by contradiction.\\\"\"\n      ]),\n      \n      h2(\"4.2 Prompt Design\"),\n      p(\"We developed a structured prompt template for concept annotation:\"),\n      p([\n        { text: \"\\\"You are a Wikidata concept annotation expert. Analyze the following text and identify all concepts that can be linked to Wikidata. Requirements: (1) Identify explicit concepts (entities directly mentioned in the text), (2) Identify implicit concepts (methods, principles, patterns that the text embodies but does not explicitly name), (3) Use standard English concept names as they appear in Wikidata, (4) Classify by hierarchy: methodology > domain concept > specific entity.\\\"\", italics: true }\n      ]),\n      p(\"The prompt requests JSON-formatted output separating explicit and implicit concepts, with evidence fields explaining the inference basis for implicit concepts.\"),\n      \n      h2(\"4.3 Evaluation Metrics\"),\n      p(\"We evaluate system outputs on four dimensions:\"),\n      p([\n        { text: \"Implicit Concept Recall (40%): \", bold: true },\n        \"Proportion of expected implicit concepts correctly identified. This is weighted highest as it directly measures the core capability.\"\n      ]),\n      p([\n        { text: \"Concept Precision (30%): \", bold: true },\n        \"Whether identified concepts use standard Wikidata-compatible names (verifiable via wbsearchentities API).\"\n      ]),\n      p([\n        { text: \"Explicit Concept Coverage (20%): \", bold: true },\n        \"Completeness of explicit entity identification (baseline capability).\"\n      ]),\n      p([\n        { text: \"Over-inference Penalty (10%): \", bold: true },\n        \"Deduction for concepts inferred beyond textual evidence (e.g., inferring historical context not present in text).\"\n      ]),\n      \n      h2(\"4.4 Ground Truth Establishment\"),\n      p(\"For each test case, we established ground truth implicit concepts through:\"),\n      p(\"(1) Domain expert identification of the phenomenon/method described\"),\n      p(\"(2) Verification that the concept exists in Wikidata with a Q-identifier\"),\n      p(\"(3) Confirmation that the concept's standard name does not appear in the test text\"),\n      p([\n        \"For example, T1's ground truth includes Q184899 (proof by contradiction)\u2014verified to exist in Wikidata, with the standard name absent from the proof text.\"\n      ]),\n\n      // 5. Then 2q\u00b2 = p\u00b2, so p\u00b2 is even, thus p is even. Let p = 2k, then q\u00b2 = 2k\u00b2, so q is also even. But this contradicts our assumption that p and q are coprime. LLM parameter compression loses these low-frequency arbitrary mappings.\"\n      ]),\n\n      new Paragraph({ children: [new PageBreak()] }),\n      \n      // 6. Discussion\n      h1(\"6. Discussion\"),\n      \n      h2(\"6.1 Why LLMs Succeed at Implicit Concept Recognition\"),\n      p(\"Our results demonstrate that LLMs effectively recognize implicit concepts despite this task being unsolved by traditional NLP systems. We attribute this to three factors:\"),\n      p([\n        { text: \"Pattern Recognition Training: \", bold: true },\n        \"LLMs are trained on vast corpora where concepts are repeatedly instantiated alongside their names. A proof by contradiction has been described thousands of times in training data, teaching the model the structural pattern.\"\n      ]),\n      p([\n        { text: \"Semantic Compression: \", bold: true },\n        \"Unlike explicit entity linking (which requires specific string matching), implicit concept recognition requires understanding abstract patterns\u2014precisely what neural network representation learning achieves.\"\n      ]),\n      p([\n        { text: \"Cross-Domain Transfer: \", bold: true },\n        \"The concept \\\"feedback loop\\\" appears in economics, biology, engineering, and control theory. While LLMs see \\\"Q937\\\" associated with Einstein in training data, the association strength depends entirely on frequency. Abstract concepts appear with their Q-identifiers far less often than famous entities.\"\n      ]),\n      p([\n        { text: \"Compression Artifacts: \", bold: true },\n        \"LLM parameters compress training data lossy-ly. High-frequency patterns survive compression; low-frequency arbitrary mappings are lost or confused with similar mappings.\"\n      ]),\n      \n      h2(\"6.3 The Hybrid Architecture\"),\n      p(\"Our findings support a clear architectural recommendation:\"),\n      p([\n        \"LLM (semantic understanding) \u2192 Concept Names \u2192 Wikidata API (wbsearchentities) \u2192 Q-identifiers\"\n      ], { alignment: AlignmentType.CENTER }),\n      new Paragraph({ spacing: { after: 200 } }),\n      \n      p(\"This architecture leverages each component's strength:\"),\n      p([\n        \"\u2022 \", { text: \"LLMs: \", bold: true }, \"Excel at recognizing what concepts texts embody (semantic pattern matching)\"\n      ]),\n      p([\n        \"\u2022 \", { text: \"APIs: \", bold: true }, \"Excel at precise identifier lookup (exact string matching against authoritative databases)\"\n      ]),\n      p(\"Attempting to make LLMs do both tasks produces unreliable results on the second.\"),\n      \n      h2(\"6.4 Implications for Knowledge Graph Construction\"),\n      p(\"This work has significant implications for automated knowledge graph population:\"),\n      p([\n        { text: \"Expanded Coverage: \", bold: true },\n        \"Current systems miss implicit concepts entirely. Larger-scale evaluation is needed.\"\n      ]),\n      p([\n        { text: \"Edge Cases: \", bold: true },\n        \"We selected canonical examples of each concept. Performance on ambiguous or hybrid cases (e.g., proofs using multiple methods) requires further study.\"\n      ]),\n      p([\n        { text: \"Explicit/Implicit Boundary: \", bold: true },\n        \"The classification showed occasional disagreements (e.g., whether \\\"quicksort\\\" is explicit when \\\"pivot\\\" is mentioned). Clearer operationalization needed.\"\n      ]),\n      p([\n        { text: \"Over-inference: \", bold: true },\n        \"LLMs sometimes inferred concepts beyond textual evidence. Calibrating inference depth remains an open problem.\"\n      ]),\n\n      // 7. Conclusion\n      h1(\"7. Conclusion\"),\n      p([\n        \"This paper introduced the Implicit Concept Recognition Problem\u2014linking texts to knowledge base concepts they embody but do not explicitly name\u2014and demonstrated that Large Language Models provide an effective solution to the semantic recognition component of this task.\"\n      ]),\n      p(\"Our key findings are:\"),\n      p([\n        { text: \"1. \", bold: true },\n        \"LLMs achieve 100% recall on core implicit concepts across diverse domains (mathematics, algorithms, economics, game theory, physics).\"\n      ]),\n      p([\n        { text: \"2. \", bold: true },\n        \"LLMs cannot reliably produce knowledge base identifiers (Q-numbers) directly, as these are arbitrary codes not derivable through reasoning.\"\n      ]),\n      p([\n        { text: \"3. \", bold: true },\n        \"A hybrid architecture (LLM \u2192 Concept Names \u2192 API \u2192 Identifiers) combines semantic understanding with identifier precision effectively.\"\n      ]),\n      p([\n        \"This work opens new research directions in comprehensive text-to-knowledge-graph linking. Future work should address scaling to larger benchmarks, handling ambiguous cases, and integrating implicit concept recognition into end-to-end knowledge graph construction pipelines.\"\n      ]),\n      p([\n        \"The broader implication is that the boundary of knowledge graph linking can be pushed beyond explicit mentions. Texts communicate meaning not just through what they say but through what they demonstrate, embody, and instantiate. LLMs, trained to understand these semantic patterns, provide the missing capability to capture this implicit layer of meaning.\"\n      ]),\n\n      // References\n      h1(\"References\"),\n      p([\n        \"De Cao, N., Izacard, G., Riedel, S., & Petroni, F. Through systematic experiments across five domains (mathematics, algorithms, economics, game theory, and quantum physics), we demonstrate that LLMs achieve 100% recall on core implicit concepts while maintaining 96% overall accuracy. We propose a hybrid architecture where LLMs perform semantic concept recognition and knowledge base APIs provide precise identifier mapping. Our findings reveal that the bottleneck in concept-to-knowledge-graph linking lies not in semantic understanding\\u2014which LLMs handle effectively\\u2014but in arbitrary identifier recall, which API lookup resolves. AIDA (Hoffart et al., 2011) pioneered joint entity disambiguation using coherence graphs. More recent neural approaches include BLINK (Wu et al., 2020), which uses bi-encoder architecture for candidate retrieval, and mGENRE (De Cao et al., 2022), which generates entity names autoregressively across 100+ languages. FALCON 2.0 (Sakor et al., 2020) specifically targets Wikidata, combining rule-based and neural methods for entity and relation linking.\\\"\\n      ]),\\n      p([\\n        \\\"Despite these advances, all existing systems share a common assumption: the target concept must have a \\\",\\n        italic(\\\"surface form\\\"),\\n        \\\" in the text that can be matched against knowledge base entries. However, these tasks focus on \\\",\\n        italic(\\\"extracting stated facts\\\"),\\n        \\\" rather than \\\",\\n        italic(\\\"inferring unstated concepts\\\"),\\n        \\\".\\\"\\n      ]),\\n      \\n      h2(\\\"2.4 The Gap: Implicit Concept Recognition\\\"),\\n      p([\\n        \\\"To our knowledge, no existing system addresses the task of linking texts to concepts they \\\",\\n        italic(\\\"embody but do not name\\\"),\\n        \\\". This gap is significant because much of what texts communicate is implicit\\u2014a proof demonstrates a method, a narrative illustrates a pattern, a description instantiates a principle. Current NLP systems miss this semantic layer entirely.\\\"\\n      ]),\\n\\n      // 3. Problem Formulation\\n      h1(\\\"3. Example: The \\u221a2 proof above embodies \\\\\\\"proof by contradiction\\\\\\\" (Q184899) without mentioning it.\\\"\\n      ]),\\n      p(\\\"This distinction is not merely terminological\\u2014it reflects fundamentally different recognition mechanisms:\\\"),\\n      \\n      createTable(\\n        [\\\"Dimension\\\", \\\"Explicit Concepts\\\", \\\"Implicit Concepts\\\"],\\n        [\\n          [\\\"Recognition mechanism\\\", \\\"Surface form matching\\\", \\\"Semantic pattern recognition\\\"],\\n          [\\\"Knowledge required\\\", \\\"Mention-entity dictionary\\\", \\\"Conceptual understanding\\\"],\\n          [\\\"Current system capability\\\", \\\"High (solved problem)\\\", \\\"Near zero (unsolved)\\\"],\\n          [\\\"Wikidata examples\\\", \\\"Q937 (Einstein)\\\", \\\"Q184899 (proof by contradiction)\\\"]\\n        ]\\n      ),\\n      new Paragraph({ spacing: { after: 200 } }),\\n      \\n      h2(\\\"3.2 The Identifier Precision Problem\\\"),\\n      p(\\\"A secondary challenge emerges even when implicit concepts are recognized: mapping concept names to precise knowledge base identifiers. LLM parameter compression loses these low-frequency arbitrary mappings.\\\"\\n      ]),\\n\\n      new Paragraph({ children: [new PageBreak()] }),\\n      \\n      // 6. Discussion\\n      h1(\\\"6. Discussion\\\"),\\n      \\n      h2(\\\"6.1 Why LLMs Succeed at Implicit Concept Recognition\\\"),\\n      p(\\\"Our results demonstrate that LLMs effectively recognize implicit concepts despite this task being unsolved by traditional NLP systems. We attribute this to three factors:\\\"),\\n      p([\\n        { text: \\\"Pattern Recognition Training: \\\", bold: true },\\n        \\\"LLMs are trained on vast corpora where concepts are repeatedly instantiated alongside their names. A proof by contradiction has been described thousands of times in training data, teaching the model the structural pattern.\\\"\\n      ]),\\n      p([\\n        { text: \\\"Semantic Compression: \\\", bold: true },\\n        \\\"Unlike explicit entity linking (which requires specific string matching), implicit concept recognition requires understanding abstract patterns\\u2014precisely what neural network representation learning achieves.\\\"\\n      ]),\\n      p([\\n        { text: \\\"Cross-Domain Transfer: \\\", bold: true },\\n        \\\"The concept \\\\\\\"feedback loop\\\\\\\" appears in economics, biology, engineering, and control theory. LLMs learn domain-agnostic conceptual patterns that transfer across fields.\\\"\\n      ]),\\n      \\n      h2(\\\"6.2 Why LLMs Fail at Identifier Recall\\\"),\\n      p(\\\"The failure to reliably produce Q-identifiers reveals a fundamental limitation:\\\"),\\n      p([\\n        { text: \\\"Arbitrary vs. Semantic Mappings: \\\", bold: true },\\n        \\\"The mapping from \\\\\\\"Einstein\\\\\\\" to Q937 has no semantic basis\\u2014it is pure memorization. While LLMs see \\\\\\\"Q937\\\\\\\" associated with Einstein in training data, the association strength depends entirely on frequency. Abstract concepts appear with their Q-identifiers far less often than famous entities.\\\"\\n      ]),\\n      p([\\n        { text: \\\"Compression Artifacts: \\\", bold: true },\\n        \\\"LLM parameters compress training data lossy-ly. High-frequency patterns survive compression; low-frequency arbitrary mappings are lost or confused with similar mappings.\\\"\\n      ]),\\n      \\n      h2(\\\"6.3 The Hybrid Architecture\\\"),\\n      p(\\\"Our findings support a clear architectural recommendation:\\\"),\\n      p([\\n        \\\"LLM (semantic understanding) \\u2192 Concept Names \\u2192 Wikidata API (wbsearchentities) \\u2192 Q-identifiers\\\"\\n      ], { alignment: AlignmentType.CENTER }),\\n      new Paragraph({ spacing: { after: 200 } }),\\n      \\n      p(\\\"This architecture leverages each component's strength:\\\"),\\n      p([\\n        \\\"\\u2022 \\\", { text: \\\"LLMs: \\\", bold: true }, \\\"Excel at recognizing what concepts texts embody (semantic pattern matching)\\\"\\n      ]),\\n      p([\\n        \\\"\\u2022 \\\", { text: \\\"APIs: \\\", bold: true }, \\\"Excel at precise identifier lookup (exact string matching against authoritative databases)\\\"\\n      ]),\\n      p(\\\"Attempting to make LLMs do both tasks produces unreliable results on the second.\\\"),\\n      \\n      h2(\\\"6.4 Implications for Knowledge Graph Construction\\\"),\\n      p(\\\"This work has significant implications for automated knowledge graph population:\\\"),\\n      p([\\n        { text: \\\"Expanded Coverage: \\\", bold: true },\\n        \\\"Current systems miss implicit concepts entirely. Our approach enables linking texts not just to entities they mention but to concepts they demonstrate.\\\"\\n      ]),\\n      p([\\n        { text: \\\"Richer Semantic Indexing: \\\", bold: true },\\n        \\\"A mathematical proof can now be indexed under \\\\\\\"proof by contradiction\\\\\\\" even if those words never appear\\u2014enabling semantic search across methodologies.\\\"\\n      ]),\\n      p([\\n        { text: \\\"Cross-Document Linking: \\\", bold: true },\\n        \\\"Documents embodying the same implicit concept (e.g., multiple prisoner's dilemma scenarios) can be linked through shared conceptual structure.\\\"\\n      ]),\\n      \\n      h2(\\\"6.5 Limitations\\\"),\\n      p(\\\"Our study has several limitations:\\\"),\\n      p([\\n        { text: \\\"Scale: \\\", bold: true },\\n        \\\"Five test cases across five domains provide proof of concept but not comprehensive coverage. Larger-scale evaluation is needed.\\\"\\n      ]),\\n      p([\\n        { text: \\\"Edge Cases: \\\", bold: true },\\n        \\\"We selected canonical examples of each concept. Performance on ambiguous or hybrid cases (e.g., proofs using multiple methods) requires further study.\\\"\\n      ]),\\n      p([\\n        { text: \\\"Explicit/Implicit Boundary: \\\", bold: true },\\n        \\\"The classification showed occasional disagreements (e.g., whether \\\\\\\"quicksort\\\\\\\" is explicit when \\\\\\\"pivot\\\\\\\" is mentioned). Clearer operationalization needed.\\\"\\n      ]),\\n      p([\\n        { text: \\\"Over-inference: \\\", bold: true },\\n        \\\"LLMs sometimes inferred concepts beyond textual evidence. Calibrating inference depth remains an open problem.\\\"\\n      ]),\\n\\n      // 7. Conclusion\\n      h1(\\\"7. Conclusion\\\"),\\n      p([\\n        \\\"This paper introduced the Implicit Concept Recognition Problem\\u2014linking texts to knowledge base concepts they embody but do not explicitly name\\u2014and demonstrated that Large Language Models provide an effective solution to the semantic recognition component of this task.\\\"\\n      ]),\\n      p(\\\"Our key findings are:\\\"),\\n      p([\\n        { text: \\\"1. \\\", bold: true },\\n        \\\"LLMs achieve 100% recall on core implicit concepts across diverse domains (mathematics, algorithms, economics, game theory, physics).\\\"\\n      ]),\\n      p([\\n        { text: \\\"2. \\\", bold: true },\\n        \\\"LLMs cannot reliably produce knowledge base identifiers (Q-numbers) directly, as these are arbitrary codes not derivable through reasoning.\\\"\\n      ]),\\n      p([\\n        { text: \\\"3. \\\", bold: true },\\n        \\\"A hybrid architecture (LLM \\u2192 Concept Names \\u2192 API \\u2192 Identifiers) combines semantic understanding with identifier precision effectively.\\\"\\n      ]),\\n      p([\\n        \\\"This work opens new research directions in comprehensive text-to-knowledge-graph linking. Future work should address scaling to larger benchmarks, handling ambiguous cases, and integrating implicit concept recognition into end-to-end knowledge graph construction pipelines.\\\"\\n      ]),\\n      p([\\n        \\\"The broader implication is that the boundary of knowledge graph linking can be pushed beyond explicit mentions. Texts communicate meaning not just through what they say but through what they demonstrate, embody, and instantiate. LLMs, trained to understand these semantic patterns, provide the missing capability to capture this implicit layer of meaning.\\\"\\n      ]),\\n\\n      // References\\n      h1(\\\"References\\\"),\\n      p([\\n        \\\"De Cao, N., Izacard, G., Riedel, S., & Petroni, F. \u9690\u5f0f\u6982\u5ff5\n\n\u6211\u4eec\u533a\u5206\u4e24\u7c7b\u6587\u672c-\u6982\u5ff5\u5173\u7cfb\uff1a\n\n**\u663e\u5f0f\u6982\u5ff5**\uff1a\u5982\u679c\u6587\u672cT\u5305\u542b\u60ef\u5e38\u6307\u5411\u6982\u5ff5C\u7684\u8868\u9762\u5f62\u5f0fS\uff0c\u5219\u6982\u5ff5C\u5728\u6587\u672cT\u4e2d\u662f\u663e\u5f0f\u7684\u3002\u4f8b\u5982\uff1a\"\u7231\u56e0\u65af\u5766\u53d1\u5c55\u4e86\u76f8\u5bf9\u8bba\"\u4e2d\u7684\"\u7231\u56e0\u65af\u5766\"\u663e\u5f0f\u6307\u5411Q937\u3002\n\n**\u9690\u5f0f\u6982\u5ff5**\uff1a\u5982\u679c\u6587\u672cT\u5b9e\u4f8b\u5316\u3001\u5c55\u793a\u6216\u4f53\u73b0\u6982\u5ff5C\uff0c\u4f46\u4e0d\u5305\u542bC\u7684\u4efb\u4f55\u60ef\u5e38\u8868\u9762\u5f62\u5f0f\uff0c\u5219\u6982\u5ff5C\u5728\u6587\u672cT\u4e2d\u662f\u9690\u5f0f\u7684\u3002\u4f8b\u5982\uff1a\u4e0a\u8ff0\u221a2\u8bc1\u660e\u4f53\u73b0\u4e86\"\u53cd\u8bc1\u6cd5\"\uff08Q184899\uff09\u4f46\u672a\u63d0\u53ca\u5b83\u3002\n\n\u8fd9\u4e00\u533a\u5206\u4e0d\u4ec5\u4ec5\u662f\u672f\u8bed\u4e0a\u7684\u2014\u2014\u5b83\u53cd\u6620\u4e86\u6839\u672c\u4e0d\u540c\u7684\u8bc6\u522b\u673a\u5236\uff1a\n\n| 3.3 4.2 \u63d0\u793a\u8bbe\u8ba1\n\n\u6211\u4eec\u5f00\u53d1\u4e86\u4e00\u4e2a\u7ed3\u6784\u5316\u7684\u6982\u5ff5\u6807\u6ce8\u63d0\u793a\u6a21\u677f\uff1a\n\n> 4.4 \u771f\u503c\u5efa\u7acb\n\n\u5bf9\u4e8e\u6bcf\u4e2a\u6d4b\u8bd5\u6848\u4f8b\uff0c\u6211\u4eec\u901a\u8fc7\u4ee5\u4e0b\u65b9\u5f0f\u5efa\u7acb\u771f\u503c\u9690\u5f0f\u6982\u5ff5\uff1a\n\n1. 5. \u5b9e\u9a8c\u4e0e\u7ed3\u679c\n\n### 5.1 \u6d4b\u8bd5\u6848\u4f8b\u7ed3\u679c\n\n\u6211\u4eec\u5c55\u793a\u6bcf\u4e2a\u6d4b\u8bd5\u6848\u4f8b\u7684\u8be6\u7ec6\u7ed3\u679c\uff1a\n\n#### \u6d4b\u8bd51\uff1a\u6570\u5b66\u8bc1\u660e\uff08\u221a2\u65e0\u7406\u6027\uff09\n\n**\u8f93\u5165**\uff1a\"\u5047\u8bbe\u221a2 = p/q\uff0c\u5176\u4e2dp\u548cq\u4e92\u8d28\u3002\u52192q\u00b2 = \u6d4b\u8bd52\uff1a\u7b97\u6cd5\u63cf\u8ff0\uff08\u5feb\u901f\u6392\u5e8f\uff09\n\n**\u8f93\u5165**\uff1a\"\u8981\u5bf9\u6570\u7ec4\u6392\u5e8f\uff0c\u9009\u53d6\u4e00\u4e2a\u67a2\u8f74\u5143\u7d20\u3002\u5c06\u6570\u7ec4\u5206\u533a\uff0c\u4f7f\u5c0f\u4e8e\u67a2\u8f74\u7684\u5143\u7d20\u5728\u5de6\u8fb9\uff0c\u5927\u4e8e\u67a2\u8f74\u7684\u5728\u53f3\u8fb9\u3002\u7136\u540e\u9012\u5f52\u5730\u5bf9\u5de6\u53f3\u5b50\u6570\u7ec4\u5e94\u7528\u76f8\u540c\u7684\u8fc7\u7a0b\uff0c\u76f4\u5230\u6bcf\u4e2a\u5b50\u6570\u7ec4\u53ea\u6709\u4e00\u4e2a\u5143\u7d20\u3002\"\n\n**\u9884\u671f\u9690\u5f0f\u6982\u5ff5**\uff1a\u5feb\u901f\u6392\u5e8f\u3001\u5206\u6cbb\u7b97\u6cd5\u3001\u9012\u5f52\u3001\u5206\u533a\n\n**LLM\u8f93\u51fa\uff08\u9690\u5f0f\uff09**\uff1a\u5feb\u901f\u6392\u5e8f 6. \u8ba8\u8bba\n\n### 6.1 6.2 \u8bed\u4e49\u6620\u5c04**\uff1a\u4ece\"\u7231\u56e0\u65af\u5766\"\u5230Q937\u7684\u6620\u5c04\u6ca1\u6709\u8bed\u4e49\u57fa\u7840\u2014\u2014\u8fd9\u662f\u7eaf\u7cb9\u7684\u8bb0\u5fc6\u3002\u867d\u7136LLM\u5728\u8bad\u7ec3\u6570\u636e\u4e2d\u770b\u5230\"Q937\"\u4e0e\u7231\u56e0\u65af\u5766\u5173\u8054\uff0c\u4f46\u5173\u8054\u5f3a\u5ea6\u5b8c\u5168\u53d6\u51b3\u4e8e\u9891\u7387\u3002\u62bd\u8c61\u6982\u5ff5\u4e0e\u5176Q\u6807\u8bc6\u7b26\u4e00\u8d77\u51fa\u73b0\u7684\u9891\u7387\u8fdc\u4f4e\u4e8e\u8457\u540d\u5b9e\u4f53\u3002\n\n**\u538b\u7f29\u4f2a\u5f71**\uff1aLLM\u53c2\u6570\u6709\u635f\u538b\u7f29\u8bad\u7ec3\u6570\u636e\u3002\u9ad8\u9891\u6a21\u5f0f\u5728\u538b\u7f29\u4e2d\u5b58\u6d3b\uff1b\u4f4e\u9891\u7684\u4efb\u610f\u6620\u5c04\u4e22\u5931\u6216\u4e0e\u76f8\u4f3c\u6620\u5c04\u6df7\u6dc6\u3002\n\n### 6.3 \u6df7\u5408\u67b6\u6784\n\n\u6211\u4eec\u7684\u53d1\u73b0\u652f\u6301\u4e00\u4e2a\u660e\u786e\u7684\u67b6\u6784\u5efa\u8bae\uff1a\n\n```\nLLM\uff08\u8bed\u4e49\u7406\u89e3\uff09\u2192 \u6982\u5ff5\u540d\u79f0 \u2192 Wikidata API\uff08wbsearchentities\uff09\u2192 Q\u6807\u8bc6\u7b26\n```\n\n\u8fd9\u4e00\u67b6\u6784\u5229\u7528\u6bcf\u4e2a\u7ec4\u4ef6\u7684\u4f18\u52bf\uff1a\n\n- **LLM**\uff1a\u64c5\u957f\u8bc6\u522b\u6587\u672c\u4f53\u73b0\u7684\u6982\u5ff5\uff08\u8bed\u4e49\u6a21\u5f0f\u5339\u914d\uff09\n- **API**\uff1a\u64c5\u957f\u7cbe\u786e\u6807\u8bc6\u7b26\u67e5\u8be2\uff08\u5bf9\u6743\u5a01\u6570\u636e\u5e93\u7684\u7cbe\u786e\u5b57\u7b26\u4e32\u5339\u914d\uff09\n\n\u8bd5\u56fe\u8ba9LLM\u540c\u65f6\u5b8c\u6210\u4e24\u9879\u4efb\u52a1\u4f1a\u5728\u7b2c\u4e8c\u9879\u4e0a\u4ea7\u751f\u4e0d\u53ef\u9760\u7684\u7ed3\u679c\u3002\n\n### 6.4 \u5bf9\u77e5\u8bc6\u56fe\u8c31\u6784\u5efa\u7684\u542f\u793a\n\n\u8fd9\u9879\u5de5\u4f5c\u5bf9\u81ea\u52a8\u5316\u77e5\u8bc6\u56fe\u8c31\u586b\u5145\u6709\u91cd\u8981\u542f\u793a\uff1a\n\n**\u6269\u5c55\u8986\u76d6\u8303\u56f4**\uff1a\u5f53\u524d\u7cfb\u7edf\u5b8c\u5168\u9519\u8fc7\u9690\u5f0f\u6982\u5ff5\u3002\u6211\u4eec\u7684\u65b9\u6cd5\u4e0d\u4ec5\u80fd\u5c06\u6587\u672c\u94fe\u63a5\u5230\u5b83\u4eec\u63d0\u53ca\u7684\u5b9e\u4f53\uff0c\u8fd8\u80fd\u94fe\u63a5\u5230\u5b83\u4eec\u5c55\u793a\u7684\u6982\u5ff5\u3002\n\n**\u66f4\u4e30\u5bcc\u7684\u8bed\u4e49\u7d22\u5f15**\uff1a\u5373\u4f7f\"\u53cd\u8bc1\u6cd5\"\u8fd9\u4e9b\u8bcd\u4ece\u672a\u51fa\u73b0\uff0c\u6570\u5b66\u8bc1\u660e\u73b0\u5728\u4e5f\u53ef\u4ee5\u5728\"\u53cd\u8bc1\u6cd5\"\u4e0b\u7d22\u5f15\u2014\u2014\u5b9e\u73b0\u8de8\u65b9\u6cd5\u8bba\u7684\u8bed\u4e49\u641c\u7d22\u3002\n\n**\u8de8\u6587\u6863\u94fe\u63a5**\uff1a\u4f53\u73b0\u76f8\u540c\u9690\u5f0f\u6982\u5ff5\u7684\u6587\u6863\uff08\u4f8b\u5982\uff0c\u591a\u4e2a\u56da\u5f92\u56f0\u5883\u573a\u666f\uff09\u53ef\u4ee5\u901a\u8fc7\u5171\u4eab\u7684\u6982\u5ff5\u7ed3\u6784\u94fe\u63a5\u3002\n\n### 6.5
